INTRODUCTORY NOTE 


Tm, the first edition in Roman characters of the sixth book 
of the Pali Abhidhamma-Pitaka, has occupied several pens at 
intervals during the last five years. Divided in the Siamese 
and Burmese printed editions into three volumes, it is here 
condensed into two, the inordinate length of the seventh 
chapter, the Anusaya-Yamaka, as well as of the tenth, 
the Indriya-Yamaka, rendering the length of the volumes 
unequal, The firat seven chapters were transcribed from 
the Siumese edition by my sister, M. C. Foley; the last 
threa by my friends, Cecilia Dibben and May Smith. Their 
MS. has been collated throughout by my co-editors, M. C. 
Foley and Mabel Hunt, with the Burmese Hanthawaddy 
Press edition, and with a paper MS. copied for Professor 
Rhys Davids in Ceylon—alas! that we must still depend 
for our Sinhalese sources on these unrevised copies in 
manuscript. 

The length of the work is very considerable, being only 
exceeded, in its own Pitska, by that of the Petthüna or 
Great Book. Buddhaghosa even finds it, when expanded 
or uncondensed, in its 2,000 sections for recitation, endless 
and immeasurable’ (anantam-aparimanay, Atthasálini, 9). 
The scheme of the work, however, lends itself well to con- 
densation without sacrifice of substance or of intelligible- 
ness. The ten title-worde—mdla, khandha, eto., and their 
species—are repeated eo often and so symmetrically, that 
they have been abbreviated as soon as their place in the 
scheme has become clear. The same plan has been adopted 
for constantly recurring words in constant formulas. 

But, besides this, we have often added to the number of 
passages excised, as being repetitions, by the Siamese and 

D 3 


x Introductory Note 


Burmese editions. The structure is very symmetrical, and 
reference is directed by numbered paragraphs. The plan, 
we are aware, haa ita drawbacks. These cut-shorte—to use 
& Cheshire term—will baffle and irritate anyone dipping 
into the middle of things, or making a hurried reference ; 
but we hope and believe they will be clear enough, if 
reference be made by starting from the beginning of the 
Yamaka required, or at least from the beginning of a 
võra. 

We had hoped, by these abbreviations, to publish the 
whole work in one volume, but this proved impracticable. 
By compressing the three volumes into two, however, the 
completion of the Society's Tipitaka edition has been 
accelerated, and, with that, the publication of other texts 
now in preparation. It should not be forgotten — the 
Yamaka will recall it soon enough !—that we are not here 
dealing with the relatively readable matter of Suttanta 
literature. There such excisions and references may be 
advisable, in case of long or frequent repetition, on the 
ground of cutting expense in printing; but the mathetic 
effect—uaing the term to include moral and intellectual 
emotion—ie disastrous. The reader is baffled and chilled. 
The Yamaka, however, is no fit work for either reading or 
‘recitation’ (bhdnavara). It is possible that Buddhaghosa 
used the word in its first intention, and that paragraphs 
were read out periodically in viharas where Abhidhamma 
was regularly taught; and, indeed, the paragraphs lend 
themselves admirably to the intoning droning of regular 
mechanical routine of that kind. But to be an effective 
educational instrument the Yamaka’s only chance ie to be 
used (1) as a work of reference; (2) as a thesaurus of 
theses, from which a teacher may select, and by which he 
may expound, like a preacher with his ‘ text.’ 

When first compiled, the work was either orally and 
mentally registered only, for both teacher and pupil, or it 
may have been committed to writing only as a scheme of 
key-words with titles of procedure—annlomay, patilomay, 
ete. But even when it was committed in full to palm-leat 


Introductory Note xi 


manuscript, probably only the teacher would refer to this. 
And for his graduate knowledge it would not matter if the 
theses were set down very concisely and in digest. Each 
term would be bodied out with more or less full intension 
in his mind, and thia he could proceed to unfold. 

An interesting example of how the skeleton questions 
and answers in the opening of the second Yamaka may be 
clothed with meaning, is given by my colleague, Mr. 8. Z. 
Aung, in the ‘Compendium of Philosophy’ (Abhidhammat- 
thasangaha), p. 272, when discussing the wide implicationa 
of the term тра, The texts selected from the Khandha- 
Yamaka are from p. 16 of this volume: 1. Hüpay rüpak- 
khandho ti? Piyari paꝝ айдатйраў vüpay, na rüpakkhandho ; 
rüpakkhandho тйрай ceva ripakkhandho ca . . . 9. Na 
rüpakkhandho по rüpan ti? Piyarüpa:y sdtariipay na rüpak- 
khandho, rilpay ; тйрай ca vüpakkhandhas ca thapetva, avasesá 
па cera rüpag na ea rüpakkhandho. The teacher's exposi- 
tion of this, put into English, with only the barest 
amplification, follows: Does everytbing that we call rapa 
belong to the “material group” (or bodily organism)? 
Those eighty-one classes of consciousness got in sensuous 
experience which we call “ attractive ” тйра and “ pleasant 
rijpa—tbese are vipa, but they do not belong to the rūpa 
which is known as (our body or) the ““ material group.” 
But the twenty-eight material qualities making up that 
material group—these are both called ripa and belong also 
to the * material group." 3. Again, is anything that does 
not belong to the material group” ever called rapa? 
Things attractive and desirable are called rapa, though 
they do not belong to the material group. Those things 
and that group apart (or excepted), all the rest of our 
experience is neither called ripa nor does it go to make up 
the material group.’ 

Here it is obvious that a teacher might elaborate these 
two texts to & much greater extent. And it will doubtless 
le conceded that in the hands, not of a droning reciter, 
lut of an earnest expositor, even these ten ‘ valleys of dry 
hones’ inight be made living and breathing vehicles of 


xii Introductory Note 


doctrine. We may note, for example, while pp. 16, 17 are 
yet open before ua, that whereas in the third and fourth 
groups ог khandhas, there is the same absence of co- 
incidence between all that is called зайла and sankhàrd and 
the content (Inhalt) of the groups во named, as was shown 
in the case of rüpa, it is otherwise with the remaining 
khandhas. As we should express it, rüpa, sanad, sankhara, 
are generio terms including the species called ‘material 
factor and mental factor (or group) But in the case of 
feeling and consciousness (vedand, rififidna), the extension 
of these two terms coincides with that of the two mental 
groups so named. There is no feeling and no conscious- 
ness but what is khandha, or constituent of a person or 
individual. This, at any rate, shows that orthodox 
Abhidhamma rejected all theorizing as to any cosmio Stoff 
of consciousness of potential, but not actual, individualiza- 
tion, euch as might occur to the reader when viüfignadhatu 
is included with the four elements and space (cf. M. iii., 
91, 62, 240). 

I have said the reader,’ meaning thereby the modern 
European reader. It may not have occurred to the 
Buddhist teacher of olden time to guard his pupils against 
what we find thus described in a modern work :—that some- 
thing which ‘ exists already, behind the scenes, coeval with 
the world . . . the one infinite Thought [note the capital Th.] 
which is the sole reality [refracted through individual 
brains] into those millions of finite streams of conscions- 
ness known to us as our private selves.’ This is how 
William James describes the theory of mental and cerebral 
life considered as the temporary channel transmitting 
something from a bigger entity.! But for us the Yamaka 
quotation serves to show that, in Buddhist doctrine, these 
milliona of organic khandha-streams used up, between 
them, all the feeling and all the consciousness or ‘ thought’ 
of the universe at any given moment, on whatever plane of 
being the khandha-bundle or individual was living.

Introductory Note xiii 


That the students of the extraordinary masa of саќе- 
chetical analysis revealed in the Yamaka could find in it no 
little defining and delimitating efficacy in the culture of 
their concepis,! can be seen even from this опе ray of light 
thrown by the Burmese tradition as acquired by Mr. Aung. 
We have to remember that Buddhism grew up in an age 
seething with theorizing and speculation, much of it very 
misty and mystical and mythical. The Sangha itself, in 
the vigour of youth, and with no sword or stake to stiffen 
orthodoxy, was bristling with a thorny outcrop—to use 
the Dipavanes metaphor—of schismatio opinion. The 
Buddhists had a plentiful crop of their own theorisings, 
included in that orthodoxy, to stake out and to protect by 
such mutual consistency of concepts as they sought to 
compass by Abhidhamma analysis. 

They would, it is true, have denied the suggestion that 
they, too, were given to speculation. When their Buttas 
mention speculative opinion, animistic speculation is in- 
evitably meant. And it is animism that their analyses are 
chiefly concerned to guard against. Moreover, they would 
have said: Our theories—of man's going and coming in 
his many rebirthe, and the Yamaka is concerned chiefly 
with these—are not mere speculations. Both terma are 
derived from the rich treasure-house of the language of 
vision. But wherens speculation is constantly associated 
with inference and dialectic, ‘theory’ is not wholly во 
bound. We hold that by the celestial eye,’ by the eye of 
insight, at ite highest power (заттарраййдуа), man can 
behold (thedomai), as with a second sight, the pageant of 
the rebirth and decease of the successive lives of an

even as your Greek beheld’ his public games, tà Olfimpika

we believe to have been such а man. Не as seer was no 
mere theorématikós, nor even theorétikos ; he was Тнебвбв, 
nor, ав such, à mere spectator at the pageant, but, in the 
fuller sense of that term, a sacred deputy, a representative 
to us of godlike powers, even of that gnêsis which you 
reserve for the divine and the superhuman. And that 
which these, our greatest, revealed to us in Abhidhamma 
or in Suttanta, that do we state and unfold. We do not 
argue with dialectic as to ite possibility. 

And во, in these pages, affirmations are made respecting 
the mental and moral equipment and experience of several 
classes of beings, with equal certainty, whether their being 
ie accessible to our more limited perception or not. The 
peychology obtaining in the ‘Pure Abodes’ seems as 
familiar as that of the Philistine puthujjana ; that in the 
Unconscious Sphere is gauged as positively as that of the 
Arahant and of one ‘in the topmost path.’ The state- 
ments are not imparted as so many ‘revelations.’ They 
are the explication of what was, or ought to be, the content, 
in the mind of an orthodox graduate, of his conception of 
each olass of puggala or individual, and of each sphere of 
rebirth (avacara) in which he has been taught to believe. 
He would have to spend some time, as an Abhidhammika, 
in learning this explication. But he knew that he was not 
to spend his time speculating about such subjects. It had 
bean ‘seen,’ and was, in the Yamaka, said. There was 
other and better work for him to do. 

It is only the Commentary that takes any steps to 
explain the explicated. For instance, when there is re- 
birth as an asaññasatta (p. 19, 1. i), a rüpakkhandha ів 
got, but not redandkkhandha, the import of ascfifiasatta 
being absence of citta : acittakatta pana. This is simple 
enough for apprehension ; nevertheless, the farther pay- 
chology on this plane is no such simple matter as might 
be supposed, while that of the Arahants is even, at times, 


Introductory Note xv 


more baffling. The Commentary itself seems to be aware 
that, even after its exposition of the method of the first two 
Yamakas, the student may not be able to walk alone. ‘He 
who even by the method given is unable to ascertain the 
meaning, should learn by waiting ona teacher and listening 
attentively." But this is small comfort to us, unless 
indeed our friende in Burmese vihüras are able to come 
forward and help us. 

As to the Commentary itself, thia is now in process of 
being edited, and will, I trust, shortly be published. I 
may have an opportunity of dealing with some of its more 
striking comments in the second volume. Here, with ita 
help, I will only add a few words on the method of the 
Yamaka. 

In the first place, as to the title: Yamaka, the twin- 
ning, or twin-ner book, is in the Commentary equated by 
yugala,a pair. And the title may refer either to each of 
the ten categories so treated or to the entire work. As to 
what each pair or twin consists of, this, says the Com- 
mentary, constitutes a threefold basis (ti-dhdtu-yamaka- 
bhavo ceditabbo) : (1) the twin attha, or double presentation 
of the matter—namely, by way of а proposition and its 
converse; (2) the twin textual method of the text, by way 
of positive and negative statements (antloma-patilomato 
paratte-pali-dhammavasena dhamma-yamakan ti); (8) the 
twin catechetical procedure by positive and negative 
questions (anuloma-patilomato pavattapucchà vasena pueclia- 
yamakan ti). 

Or it may be more correct to see, in (1), reference to the 
pairs of terms strung together throughout the work; in (2) 
reference to the mode of procedure by way of a proposition 
and its converse; or (in 8) reference to the fact that the 
questioning is so framed. It depends on whether we limit 
жота,  pafiloma to the meaning of positive and negative

assigned them, or at least nesigned to the former, in the 
titles of the võras throughout; or whether we take them, 
once at least, in their more usual signification of a forward- 
and-reverse statement. Taken in this way, anuloma- 
patilomato, applied to the presentation of the subject- 
matter, either with respect to doctrine or with respeot to 
eatechetical form, may stand, in the apparent absence of a 
Jit logical term, for the logical procedure that is во constant 
and prominent throughout the work. I trust that before 
long & good edition of the Commentary, as well as expert 
advice, may make this matter clear. 

Certainly the most impressive feature, likely to have 
formed the title to the work, is the dual grouping of 
question and converse. The world probably contains no 
other such study in the applied logic of conversion ов the 
Yamaka. We have read in the Suttas, and elsewhere in 
Abhidhamma, of all these ten categories of terma—mila‘s, 
eic. As to the rdra's, we know how essential в feature, in 
a philosophy of becoming and of causation, conceived as 
transmitted force, is the twin fact of genesis and evanes- 
eence. Again, 'understanding'—to what extent this really 
equates pariñña—and putting away, pahãna, are dis- 
ciplines broadcast throughout the Dhamma. But to sustain 
& catechism of question and converse question respecting 
ten sets of terms repeated, with slightly varying content, 
several thousand times is a very salient feature indeed; so 
salient, indeed, that, like Sisyphus, we slide down from it 
gasping in despair. And nowhere else, in these old bed- 
rock books of Abhidhamma culture, do we find that analysis 
of term and concept, so characteristic of Pali Buddhism, 
carried out by way of the logical process of conversion, aa 
it is in the Yamaka. 

There is, as we know, no process so efficacious in the 
detection of Undistributed Middle, as our textbooks call it; 
no weapon so sure in piercing the fullacy of equating а 
universal with a particular term, as that of conversion. 
The majority of our perceptions, notably those of aight, 
and of our remarks, are proceases of inference, or the 


Introductory Note xvii 


passing from one judgment to another. And in converting, 
we test the mandate delivered us by the former judgment— 
e.g., All Y is M; now does this mean that all M is Y? 
For unless it does, we cannot pass on to say, All X is Y, 
just because all X happens to be also M.“ And which of us 
has not felt grateful to the forgotten German princess for 
eliciting from her great Thera, Euler, the happy idea of 
clarifying all such logical analysis by circles? 

Now, there is certainly no evidence in the Yamaka 
that the early Abhidhammikas were equally fortunate, nor 
even that they had clearly formulated or abstracted the 
procedure so doggedly kept np through all this long work. 
But in the later Kathävatthu we do get the technical terms 
of the logic at work in that book—e.g., patidad, upanaya, 
niggaha. Hence it is conceivable that, during the period 
occupied by the compilation of the Abhidhamma-Pitaka, 
the original contribution to Indian thought, made by the 
Buddhists as founders of Indian medieval logie, may have

been taking shape. The Yamaka, to say nothing just yet 
of the Kathavatthu (on which Mr. S. Z. Aung and myself 
are engaged), ia а work of applied logic. I am not con- 
cerned to deny that it may have existed, in the early days 
of the Sangha, in a more rudimentary form. The orthodox 
claim, for the whole of the Abhidhamma (omitting the 
Kathávatthu), is that the founder himself delivered the 
substance of it in outline to the Sangha. But the evolution 
of a book like the Yamaka, worked out in detail (vitthàrito), 
may have been of gradual growth, oceupying quite a con- 
siderable time. The success of the Sakiyaputta samaņas 
had become confirmed when this compiling took place. 
The very form of a group of works like the Abhidhamma 
shows that centres of education and training һай been 
established, drawing to themselves some at least of the 
culture of the day. Such logical development and acumen 
вз were possessed by the sophists and casuists, mentioned 
in the Brahmajala-Suttanta and the Udāna, would now 
find scope in the growing Theravada teaching and literature. 

It would be во excessive assumption to say that the 
Yamaka, together with others of its six fellow-documents, 
was compiled in order to develop a new and growing logic, 
or in order to apply such a logic taught already in the 
abstract. But there is, after all, as much to warrant our 
considering the Yamaka as a series of lessons in applied 
logic as there is for us to accept the theory, recently 
advanced by an eminent scholar, that the concluding books 
of the Culla-vagga are essentially not во much a chronicle, 
more or lesa veracious, as a literary leason in the applied 


Indian Buddhism when decrepit. They are not taught in 
the Theravada, descended from Buddhaghoss, of to-day. І 
maintain, on the contrary, that in the Sutta-Pitaka we 
already see the strong logical bias which germinated in the 
Abhidhamma-Pitaka, and produced that medieval Buddhist 
logic, the flower of which Professor Vidyabhusana во ably 
expounds.

art of deportment.' There is matter of psychological, 
ethical, and eschatological interest throughout the Yamaka, 
bui the Procrustean method of exposition, testing the 
connotation of the various endowments and beings and 
realms of life, holde the contents in a grip of iron. But 
I must leave these considerations for the present. 

The method of treatment in each of the ten divisions 
tends to be threefold, there being exceptions. There is 
first a Pannattivira, or section delimitating the term-and- 
concept (pannatti or pafiiiatti covering both), divided into 
the inquiries stated only (uddesavara) and the inquiries 
repeated with their several answers (niddesarara). The 
titles here, I regret to say, are not accurately printed, as 
the Commentary, only now coming into my hands, shows 
clearly enough. Neither the Siamese nor the Burmese 
edition is always consistent in the matter. To their editors 
it was perhaps self-evident that an Uddesavara was part of 
a Pannattivaro. Certainly the ignorance of some European 
editors was not, in their minds, a matter to be made allow- 
ance for. The second and main division is the Pavattivara, 
8 term used only in the Commentary, and referring appa- 
rently not to procedure generally, but to liviny process. 
The term is frequently so ased in the Abhidhammattha- 
sangaha—‘ patisanidlhi-pavattiyuy,’ ‘pavatte tu na kifici pi 
na labbhati.’* In this connection uppada, uppajjati, means 
reappearance at a new conception, or rebirth; nirodha, 
non-reappearance. Thus the Commentary on в typical 
question (p. 41, 1, i): ‘For one who is reborn with the 
material group (more exactly, for one for whom the 
material group is reborn) is there cessation of the feeling- 
group ?’—-the answer to this question, ‘No’—denies that,

in the one moment of rebirth (ekasnin patisandhikkhate) в 
reappearance is got together with а cessation. And if I 
read the Commentary aright, the past, present, and future 
tenees, the changes on which are rung so systematically, 
positively and negatively, in these Life-segtions, refer 
always, like the Paeticca-samuppáda, to phases of rebirth.’ 
Duplicated, and combined in paire—present uppada (2), 
present-past, present-future, etc., with the corresponding 
negative questions—they give twelve alternatives. So for 
nirodlia, and again for uppéda and nirodha combined. 

So much for the individual (puggala). ` Next, there is the 
‘where ' (yattha) of rebirth, called by the Commentary the 
оказа, or opportunity. These two, the yassa (for whom ?) 
and the yattha, are then treated of together—the given 
kind of individual and the whereabouts of his kemma 
taking effect (puggalokdso)* And in both yatthaka and 
yasea-yatthaka we have the twelve alternatives repeated. 
Thus, in the twelfth of the latter (p. 49, iii) I read as 
follows: ‘To whom and where does it happen, that the 
material group not having arisen [in bie last birth], the 
feeling-group will not cease? To those immaterial ones 
reborn in the “pure abodes” the material group did not 
previously arise, and the feeling-group will not there cease ; 
to those completing life in the pare abodes, in the im- 
material plane, neither has the material group previously 
arisen, nor will the feeling-group cease. The same is 
predicated of the perception-group (saAfiakkhandha). And 
whether the not ceasing is equivalent to the not baving 
previously arisen I am unable to say. The reply, as are 
very many others, is baffling, and makes one envious of 
those who were able to wait on a teacher and listen 
attentively.’

beings) understands the category under consideration.

Introductory Note xxi 


Here, says the Commentary, we are not concerned with 
decease and rebirth, but with the procesa of the moments 
of consciousness. . . . In that knowledge which is of the 
“ paths ” that are not concerned with the world, does he, 
whose thought has Nibbana for its object, and who under- 
stands any one group,“ understand another group? 
When, it exemplifies further on, anyone in the topmost 
path, or the Arahant (who has got from Path to Fruit), 
is the class concerning which these partial inquiries are 
made, it must be remembered that, for such, compreheneion 
of the five groups is a completed function. The questions 
do not fit their case. 

Two at least of the ten parts are so far irregular in 
method that they are not divided into the foregoing three 
sections: the Müla-Yamaka has only the fret Vara; the 
Anusaya-Yamaka has a quite different series of vdras, 
the purely ethical import of the term probably requiring 
different treatment. But these superficial notes are already 
unduly prolonged. Anything in the Commentary on this 
chapter at present not accessible to me will find mention 


in the second volume. 
C. A. F. RHYS DAVIDS. 


AB8RTON-ON-MaRSRY, 
November, 1911.
